Contents

Key words:
Vilenkin groups, Vilenkin systems, Lebesgue spaces,
Weak-Lp spaces, modulus of continuity, Vilenkin-Fourier coefficients, partial sums of Vilenkin-Fourier series, Fejér means,
T
means, Nörlund means, Riesz and Nörlund logarithmic means, martingales, martingale Hardy spaces, maximal operators, strong convergence, inequalities, approximation.

Giorgi Tutberidze

Abstract

The classical Fourier Analysis has been developed in an almost unbelieved way from the first fundamental discoveries by name Fourier. Especially a number of wonderful results have been proved and new directions of such research has been developed e.g. concerning Wavelets Theory, Gabor theory, Time-Frequency Analysis, Fast Fourier Transform, Abstract Harmonic Analysis, etc. One important reason for this is that this development is not only important for improving the ”State of the art”, but also for its importance in other areas of mathematics and also for several applications (e.g. theory of signal transmission, multiplexing, filtering,
image enhancement, coding theory, digital signal processing and pattern
recognition.)

The classical theory of Fourier series deals with decomposition of a
function into sinusoidal waves. Unlike these continuous waves the Vilenkin (Walsh) functions are rectangular waves. The development of the theory of Vilenkin-Fourier series has
been strongly influenced by the classical theory of trigonometric series.
Because of this it is inevitable to compare results of Vilenkin series to
those on trigonometric series. There are many similarities between these
theories, but there exist differences also. Much of these can be explained
by modern abstract harmonic analysis, which studies orthonormal systems from the point of view of the structure of a topological group.

The aim of my thesis is to discuss, develop and apply the newest developments of this fascinating theory connected to modern harmonic analysis. In particular, we investigate some strong convergence result of partial sums of Vilenkin-Fourier series. Moreover, we derive
necessary and sufficient conditions for the modulus of continuity so that norm convergence of subsequences of Fejér means is valid. Furthermore, we
consider Riesz and Nörlund logarithmic means. It is also proved that these results are the best possible in a
special sense. As applications both some well-known and new results are pointed out.
In addition, we investigate some
T
means, which are ”inverse” summability methods of Nörlund, but only in the case when their coefficients are monotone.

The thesis contains six chapters and two appendices, one contains some basic fact concerning classical Hardy spaces, in the other one is devoted to present about Kachmarz systems. One reason for this is that it will be more convenient for the reader to compare with the classical theory and another reason is that it give us possibility to raise new open questions. It is maybe surprising that some of these open questions concern classical situation but are motivated by the results we proved in this new situation. Totally we have explicitly pointed out 30?? open questions in this book. We hope that this can stimulate the further development of this fascinating area. We now continue by describing the main content of each of the chapters.

In Chapter 1 we first present some definitions and notations, which are
crucial for our further investigations. After that we also define some
summabilitity methods and remind about some classical facts and results. We investigate some well-known results and prove new estimates for the kernels of these summabilitity methods, which are very important to prove our main results. Moreover, we define martingale Hardy spaces and construct martingales, which help us to prove sharpness of our main results in the later chapters.

Chapter 2 is devoted to investigate some new strong convergence of partial sums and Fejér means with respect to Vilenkin systems. Next, we consider convergence of subsequences of Fejér means and prove some boundedness results for them. After that we apply these results to find necessary and sufficient conditions for the modulus of continuity for which norm convergence of Fejér means hold. We also prove sharpness of all our main results in this Chapter.

In Chapter 3 we consider boundedness of maximal operators of
T
means with respect to Vilenkin systems. We also prove that results are sharp in the special sense. After that we prove some strong convergence theorems for these summablility methods. Since Fejér means, Riesz means are well-know examples of
T
means some well-known and new results are pointed out.

In Chapter 4 we consider Riesz and Nörlund logarithmic means with respect to Vilenkin systems. In particular, we prove some strong convergence theorems of Riesz means with respect to Vilenkin systems. Moreover, we also prove sharpness of this result for only Walsh-Fourier series. Next, we investigate boundedness of maximal operators of Nörlund logarithmic means. We also obtain a.e. convergence of this means.

Giorgi Tutberidze

1
Preliminaries

1.1
Vilenkin groups and Functions

Denote by
\mathbb{N}_{+}
the set of the positive integers,

\mathbb{N}:=\mathbb{N}_{+}\cup\{0\}.

Let
m:=(m_{0},m_{1},\ldots)
be a sequence of positive
integers not less than 2. Denote by

Z_{m_{k}}:=\{0,1,\ldots,m_{k}-1\}

the additive group of integers modulo
m_{k}
.

Define the group
G_{m}
as the complete direct product of the groups
Z_{m_{k}}
with the product of the discrete topologies of
Z_{m_{k}}
.

The direct product
\mu
of the measures

\mu_{k}\left(j\right):=1/m_{k}\hskip 20.00003pt(j\in Z_{m_{k}})

is the Haar measure on
G_{m}
with
\mu\left(G_{m}\right)=1.

If
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}<\infty
, then we call
G_{m}
a bounded Vilenkin group.
If the generating sequence
m
is not bounded, then
G_{m}
is said to be an unbounded Vilenkin group.

In this book we discuss bounded Vilenkin groups, i.e. the case
when
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}<\infty.

The elements of
G_{m}
are represented by sequences

x:=\left(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{j},\ldots\right)\hskip 20.00003pt\left(x_{j}\in Z_{m_{j}}\right).

Vilenkin group can be metrizable with the following metric:

d\left(x,y\right):=\left|x-y\right|:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|x_{k}-y_{k}\right|}{M_{k+1}},\hskip 20.00003pt\left(x\in G_{m}\right).

It is easy to give a base for the neighborhoods of
G_{m}:

\displaystyle I_{0}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=G_{m},

\displaystyle I_{n}(x)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\{y\in G_{m}\mid y_{0}=x_{0},\ldots,y_{n-1}=x_{n-1}\}\hskip 20.00003pt\left(x\in G_{m},\ \ n\in\mathbb{N}\right).

Let

e_{n}:=\left(0,\ldots,0,x_{n}=1,0,\ldots\right)\in G_{m}\hskip 20.00003pt\left(n\in\mathbb{N}\right).

If we define
I_{n}:=I_{n}\left(0\right),
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\overline{I_{n}}:=G_{m}\ \ \backslash

I_{n},
then

\overline{I_{N}}=\overset{N-1}{\underset{s=0}{\bigcup}}I_{s}\backslash I_{s+1}=\left(\overset{N-2}{\underset{k=0}{\bigcup}}\overset{N-1}{\underset{l=k+1}{\bigcup}}I_{N}^{k,l}\right)\bigcup\left(\underset{k=1}{\bigcup\limits^{N-1}}I_{N}^{k,N}\right),

(1.1)

where

I_{N}^{k,l}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}I_{N}(0,\ldots,0,x_{k}\neq 0,0,...,0,x_{l}\neq 0,x_{l+1},\ldots,x_{N-1},\ldots),\\
\text{for}\qquad k<l<N,\\
I_{N}(0,\ldots,0,x_{k}\neq 0,x_{k+1}=0,\ldots,x_{N-1}=0,x_{N},\ldots),\\
\text{for }\qquad l=N.\end{array}\right.

The norm (or quasi-norm when
0<p<1
) of the Lebesgue space
L_{p}(G_{m})

\left(0<p<\infty\right)
is defined by

\left\|f\right\|_{p}:=\left(\int_{G_{m}}\left|f\right|^{p}d\mu\right)^{1/p}.

The space
weak-L_{p}\left(G_{m}\right)
consists of all measurable functions
f
, for which

\left\|f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}:=\underset{\lambda>0}{\sup}\lambda\{\mu\left(f>\lambda\right)\}^{1/p}<+\infty.

The norm of the space of continuous functions
C(G_{m})
is defined by

\left\|f\right\|_{C}:=\underset{x\in G_{m}}{\sup}\left|f(x)\right|<c<\infty.

The best approximation of
f\in L_{p}(G_{m})

(1\leq p\leq\infty)
is
defined as

E_{n}\left(f,L_{p}\right):=\inf_{\psi\in\emph{P}_{n}}\left\|f-\psi\right\|_{p},

where
\emph{P}_{n}
is set of all Vilenkin polynomials of order less than

n\in\mathbb{N}
.

The modulus of continuity of functions of Lebesgue spaces
f\in L_{p}\left(G_{m}\right)
and continous functions
f\in C\left(G_{m}\right)
are defined by

\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right):=\sup\limits_{h\in I_{n}}\left\|f\left(\cdot-h\right)-f\left(\cdot\right)\right\|_{p}

and

\omega_{C}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right):=\underset{h\in I_{n}}{\sup}\left\|f\left(\cdot-h\right)-f\left(\cdot\right)\right\|_{C},

respectively.

If we define the so-called generalized number system based on
m
in the
following way :

M_{0}:=1,\ \ M_{k+1}:=m_{k}M_{k}\ \ (k\in\mathbb{N}),

then every
n\in\mathbb{N}
can be uniquely expressed as

n=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}n_{j}M_{j},

where
n_{j}\in Z_{m_{j}}\ \ (j\in\mathbb{N}_{+})
and only a finite number of
n_{j}^{{}^{\prime}}
‘s differ from zero.

Next, we introduce on
G_{m}
an orthonormal systems, which are called
Vilenkin systems.

At first, we define the complex-valued function

r_{k}\left(x\right):G_{m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C},

the generalized Rademacher functions, by

r_{k}\left(x\right):=\exp\left(2\pi ix_{k}/m_{k}\right),\ \ \left(i^{2}=-1,\ \ x\in G_{m},\ \ k\in\mathbb{N}\right).

Now, define Vilenkin systems

\psi:=(\psi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N})
on
G_{m}
as:

\psi_{n}(x):=\prod\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}r_{k}^{n_{k}}\left(x\right),\ \ \left(n\in\mathbb{N}\right).

The Vilenkin systems are orthonormal and complete in
L_{2}\left(G_{m}\right).

It is well-known that for all
n\in\mathbb{N},

\displaystyle\left|\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1,\text{ }

(1.2)

\displaystyle\psi_{n}\left(x+y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\text{\ }\psi_{n}\left(y\right),

\displaystyle\psi_{n}\left(-x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{n^{\ast}}\left(x\right)=\overline{\psi}_{n}\left(x\right),\text{ \ \ \ }

\displaystyle\psi_{n}\left(x-y\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\text{\ }\overline{\psi}_{n}\left(y\right),

\displaystyle\psi_{n\widehat{+}k}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{s}\psi_{n}\left(x\right),\text{\ \ }\left(s,n\in\mathbb{N},\text{ \ \ }x,y\in G_{m}\right).

Specifically, we call this system the Walsh-Paley system when
m\equiv 2.

1.2
partial sums and Fejér means with respect to the Vilenkin systems

Next, we introduce some analogues of the usual definitions in
Fourier-analysis. If
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right)
we can define the
Fourier coefficients, the partial sums of Vilenkin-Fourier series, the Dirichlet
kernels, Fejér means,
Dirichlet and Fejér kernels with respect to the Vilenkin system with respect to Vilenkin systems in the usual manner:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}\left(n\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\int_{G_{m}}f\overline{\psi}_{n}d\mu,\hskip 20.00003pt\left(n\in\mathbb{N}\right),

\displaystyle S_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\widehat{f}\left(k\right)\psi_{k},\hskip 20.00003pt\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}f

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}S_{k}f,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle D_{n}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\psi_{k},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle K_{n}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\overset{n-1}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}D_{k},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right).

respectively.

It is easy to see that

\displaystyle S_{n}f\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G_{m}}f\left(t\right)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\psi_{k}\left(x-t\right)d\mu\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G_{m}}f\left(t\right)D_{n}\left(x-t\right)d\mu\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f\ast D_{n}\right)\left(x\right).

It is well-known that (for details see e.g.
[
112
]
,
[
62
]
and
[
2
]
) that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq s_{n}\leq m_{n}-1
the following equalities holds

D_{j+M_{n}}=D_{M_{n}}+\psi_{M_{n}}D_{j}=D_{M_{n}}+r_{n}D_{j},\hskip 20.00003ptj\leq\left(m_{n}-1\right)M_{n},

(1.3)

\displaystyle D_{M_{n}-j}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{M_{n}}(x)-\overline{\psi}_{M_{n}-1}(-x)D_{j}(-x)

(1.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{M_{n}}(x)-\psi_{M_{n}-1}(x)\overline{D}_{j}(x),\hskip 20.00003ptj<M_{n}.

\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 0.0ptD_{M_{n}}\left(x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\text{ }M_{n}\text{ \ \ \ }x\in I_{n}\\
\text{ }0\text{ \qquad}x\notin I_{n}\end{array}\right.

(1.5)

D_{s_{n}M_{n}}=D_{M_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{s_{n}-1}\psi_{kM_{n}}=D_{M_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{s_{n}-1}r_{n}^{k}

(1.6)

and

D_{n}=\psi_{n}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}D_{M_{j}}\sum_{k=m_{j}-n_{j}}^{m_{j}-1}r_{j}^{k}\right).

(1.7)

By using (
1.5
) we immediately get that

\|D_{M_{n}}\|_{1}=1<\infty.

(1.8)

It is obvious that

\displaystyle\sigma_{n}f\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\overset{n-1}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}\left(D_{k}\ast f\right)\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{G_{m}}f\left(t\right)K_{n}\left(x-t\right)d\mu\left(t\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(f\ast K_{n}\right)\left(x\right).

where
K_{n}
are the so called Fejér kernels.

It is well-known that (for details see e.g.
[
37
]
) for every

n>t,

t,n\in\mathbb{N}
we have the following equality:

K_{M_{n}}\left(x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{M_{t}}{1-r_{t}\left(x\right)},&x\in I_{t}\backslash I_{t+1},\qquad x-x_{t}e_{t}\in I_{n},\\
\frac{M_{n}+1}{2},&x\in I_{n},\\
0,&\text{otherwise. }\end{array}\right.

(1.9)

Moreover,

s_{n}M_{n}K_{s_{n}M_{n}}=\sum_{l=0}^{s_{n}-1}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{l-1}r_{n}^{i}\right)M_{n}D_{M_{n}}+\left(\sum_{l=0}^{s_{n}-1}r_{n}^{l}\right)M_{n}K_{M_{n}}

(1.10)

and

The next equality of Fejér kernels is very important for our further investigation (for details see Blahota and Tephnadze
[
13
]
). In particular, if
n=\sum_{i=1}^{r}s_{n_{i}}M_{n_{i}}
, where

n_{1}>n_{2}>\dots>n_{r}\geq 0
and
1\leq s_{n_{i}}<m_{n_{i}}
for all

1\leq i\leq r
as well as
n^{(k)}=n-\sum_{i=1}^{k}s_{n_{i}}M_{n_{i}}
,
where
0<k\leq r
, then

nK_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{r}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}r_{n_{j}}^{s_{n_{j}}}\right)s_{n_{k}}M_{n_{k}}K_{s_{n_{k}}M_{n_{k}}}+\sum_{k=1}^{r-1}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}r_{n_{j}}^{s_{n_{j}}}\right)n^{(k)}D_{s_{n_{k}}M_{n_{k}}}.

(1.11)

It is well-known that

\|K_{n}\|_{1}<c<\infty.

(1.12)

Let define maximal operators of partial sums and Féjer means by

\displaystyle{S}^{\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|S_{n}f\right|,

\displaystyle{\sigma}^{\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\sigma_{n}f\right|.

Let define restricted maximal operators of partial sums and Féjer means by

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{\#}^{\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|S_{M_{n}}f\right|,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\sigma}_{\#}^{\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\sigma_{M_{n}}f\right|.

1.3
Character
\rho\left(n\right)
and Lebesgue constants with respect to Vilenkin Systems

Let as define

\left\langle n\right\rangle:=\min\{j\in\mathbb{N}:n_{j}\neq 0\}\text{ \ and \ }\left|n\right|:=\max\{j\in\mathbb{N}:n_{j}\neq 0\},

that is

M_{\left|n\right|}\leq n\leq M_{\left|n\right|+1}.

Set

\rho\left(n\right):=\left|n\right|-\left\langle n\right\rangle,\hskip 20.00003pt\text{for all}\hskip 20.00003ptn\in\mathbb{N}.

For the natural numbers
n=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}n_{j}M_{j}
and
k=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}k_{j}M_{j}
we define

n\widehat{+}k:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\left(n_{i}\oplus k_{i}\right)}{M_{i+1}}

and

n\widehat{-}k:=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}{\left(n_{i}\ominus k_{i}\right)}{M_{i+1}},

where

a_{i}\oplus b_{i}:=(a_{i}+b_{i})\text{mod}m_{i},\hskip 20.00003pta_{i},b_{i}\in Z_{m_{i}}

and
\ominus
is the inverse operation for
\oplus
.

For the natural number
n=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}n_{j}M_{j},
we define
functions
v
and
v^{\ast}
by

v\left(n\right):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left|\delta_{j+1}-\delta_{j}\right|+\delta_{0},\hskip 20.00003ptv^{\ast}\left(n\right):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\delta_{j}^{\ast},

where

\delta_{j}=sign\left(n_{j}\right)=sign\left(\ominus n_{j}\right)\text{\ and \ }\delta_{j}^{\ast}=\left|\ominus n_{j}-1\right|\delta_{j}.

The
n
-th Lebesgue constant is defined in the following way:

L_{n}:=\left\|D_{n}\right\|_{1}.

For the trigonometric system
it is important to note that results of Fejér and Szego, latter given in
[
136
]
an explicit formula for Lebesgue constants.
The most properties of
Lebesgue constants with respect to the Walsh-Paley system were obtained
by Fine in
[
31
]
. In (
[
112
]
, p. 34), the two-sided estimate
is proved. In
[
75
]
, Lukomskii presented the lower estimate with sharp constant
1/4
. Malykhin, Telyakovskii and Kholshchevnikova
[
78
]
(see also Astashkin and Semenov
[
4
]
) improved the estimation above and proved sharp estimate with factor 1. The first proof of (
1.13
) with some different constants can be found in Lukomskii
[
75
]
, a new and shorter proof discribed in this book which improved upper bound and provide similar lower bound can be found in
[
18
]
. In particular, for
\lambda:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and for any
n=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}n_{i}M_{i}
and
m_{n}
we have the following two sided estimate:

\frac{1}{4\lambda}v\left(n\right)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}v^{\ast}\left(n\right)\leq L_{n}\leq v\left(n\right)+v^{\ast}\left(n\right).

(1.13)

Moreover, (for details see in Memic, Simon and Tephnadze
[
79
]
)

\frac{1}{nM_{n}}\underset{k=1}{\overset{M_{n}-1}{\sum}}v\left(k\right)\geq\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}},

(1.14)

Inequality (
1.13
) immediately follows that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists an absolute constant
c,
such that

\left\|D_{n}\right\|_{1}\leq c\log n.

(1.15)

For example, if we take
q_{n_{k}}=M_{2n_{k}}+M_{2n_{k}-2}+M_{2}+M_{0}
, we have following two-sided inequality

\frac{n_{k}}{2\lambda}\leq\left\|D_{q_{n_{k}}}\right\|_{1}\leq\lambda n_{k},\ \ \ \ \ \ \lambda:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}.

(1.16)

1.4
Definition and examples of Nörlund and
T
means and its maximal
operators

Let
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a sequence of nonnegative numbers. The
n
-th Nörlund means for the Fourier series of
f
is defined by

t_{n}f:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}q_{n-k}S_{k}f,

(1.17)

where

Q_{n}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{k}.

A representation

t_{n}f\left(x\right)=\underset{G}{\int}f\left(t\right)A_{n}\left(x-t\right)d\mu\left(t\right)

plays a central role in the sequel, where

A_{n}:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}q_{n-k}D_{k}

is the so-called Nörlund kernel.

In Moore
[
81
]
(see also Tephnadze
[
149
]
) it was found necessary and suficient condition of regularity of Norlund means. In particular, if
\{q_{k}:k\geq 0\}
be a sequence of nonnegative numbers,
q_{0}>0

and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Q_{n}=\infty.

Then the summability method (
1.17
) generated by
\{q_{k}:k\geq 0\}
is regular if and only if

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{q_{n-1}}{Q_{n}}=0.

(1.18)

In addition, if sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be non-increasing, then the summability method generated by

\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is regular, but if sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be non-decreasing, then the summability method generated by
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is not always regular.

Let
\{q_{k}:k\geq 0\}
be a sequence of non-negative numbers. The
n
-th
T
means for a Fourier series of
f
is defined by

T_{n}f:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\overset{n-1}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}q_{k}S_{k}f,

(1.19)

where
Q_{n}:=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}q_{k}.

It is obvious that

T_{n}f\left(x\right)=\underset{G_{m}}{\int}f\left(t\right)F_{n}\left(x-t\right)d\mu\left(t\right),

where
F_{n}:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}q_{k}D_{k}
is called the kernel of
T
means.

We always assume that
\{q_{k}:k\geq 0\}
is a sequence of non-negative numbers and
q_{0}>0.
Then the summability method (
1.19
) generated by
\{q_{k}:k\geq 0\}
is regular if and only if
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}Q_{n}=\infty.

Let
t_{n}
be Nörlund means with monotone and bounded sequence

\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
, such that

q:=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}q_{n}>c>0.

Then, if the sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is non-decreasing, we get
that

nq_{0}\leq Q_{n}\leq nq.

In the case when the sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is non-increasing,
then

nq\leq Q_{n}\leq nq_{0}.

(1.20)

In both cases we can conclude that

\frac{q_{n-1}}{Q_{n}}=O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),\text{ \ when \ }n\rightarrow\infty.

(1.21)

One of the most well-known summability method which is example of Norlund and
T
means is Fejér means, which is given when
\{q_{k}=1:k\in\mathbb{N}\}

\sigma_{n}f:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}S_{k}f.

The
\left(C,\alpha\right)
-means (Cesàro means) of the Vilenkin-Fourier
series are defined by

\sigma_{n}^{\alpha}f:=\frac{1}{A_{n}^{\alpha}}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}A_{n-k}^{\alpha-1}S_{k}f,

where

A_{0}^{\alpha}:=0,\hskip 20.00003ptA_{n}^{\alpha}:=\frac{\left(\alpha+1\right)...\left(\alpha+n\right)}{n!},\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha\neq-1,-2,...

It is well-known that (see e.g. Zygmund
[
192
]
)

A_{n}^{\alpha}=\overset{n}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}A_{n-k}^{\alpha-1},

(1.22)

A_{n}^{\alpha}-A_{n-1}^{\alpha}=A_{n}^{\alpha-1},\ \ A_{n}^{\alpha}\backsim n^{\alpha}.

(1.23)

We also consider the ”inverse”
\left(C,\alpha\right)
-means, which is an example of a
T
-means:

U_{n}^{\alpha}f:=\frac{1}{A_{n}^{\alpha}}\overset{n-1}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}A_{k}^{\alpha-1}S_{k}f,\hskip 20.00003pt0<\alpha<1.

Let
V_{n}^{\alpha}
denote
the
T
mean, where
\left\{q_{0}=1,\ q_{k}=k^{\alpha-1}:k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right\},

that is

V_{n}^{\alpha}f:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}k^{\alpha-1}S_{k}f,\hskip 20.00003pt0<\alpha<1.

The
n
-th Nörlund logarithmic mean
L_{n}
and the Riesz logarithmic mean
R_{n}
are defined by

\displaystyle L_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{S_{k}f}{n-k},

\displaystyle R_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{S_{k}f}{k},

respectively, where

l_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{k}.

Kernels of Nörlund logarithmic mean and Riesz logarithmic mean are respectively defined by

\displaystyle P_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{D_{k}f}{n-k},

\displaystyle Y_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{D_{k}f}{k}.

It is well-known that

\displaystyle\|Y_{n}f\|_{1}<c<\infty.

(1.24)

Up to now we have considered Nörlund and
T
means in the case when the sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is bounded but now we consider Nörlund and
T
summabilities with unbounded sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\ \ \beta\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and

\log^{(\beta)}x:=\overset{\beta\text{times}}{\overbrace{\log...\log}}x.

If we define the sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
by

\left\{q_{0}=0\text{ \ and \ }q_{k}=\log^{\left(\beta\right)}k^{\alpha}:k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right\},

then we get the class of Nörlund means with non-decreasing coefficients:

\kappa_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}f:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log^{\left(\beta\right)}\left(n-k\right)^{\alpha}S_{k}f.

First we note that
\kappa_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}
are
well-defined for every

n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, if we rewrite them as:

\kappa_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}f=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\log^{\left(\beta\right)}\left(n-k\right)^{\alpha}}{Q_{n}}S_{k}f.

It is obvious that

\frac{n}{2}\log^{\left(\beta\right)}\frac{n^{\alpha}}{2^{\alpha}}\leq Q_{n}\leq n\log^{\left(\beta\right)}n^{\alpha}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{q_{n-1}}{Q_{n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{c\log^{\left(\beta\right)}\left(n-1\right)^{\alpha}}{n\log^{\left(\beta\right)}n^{\alpha}}

(1.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\rightarrow 0,\text{ \ as \ }n\rightarrow\infty.

If we define the sequence
\{q_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
by

\left\{q_{0}=0,\ q_{k}=\log^{\left(\beta\right)}k^{\alpha}:k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right\},

then we get the class of
T
means with non-decreasing coefficients:

B_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}f:=\frac{1}{Q_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log^{\left(\beta\right)}k^{\alpha}S_{k}f.

We note that
B_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}
are
well-defined for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

B_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}f=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\log^{\left(\beta\right)}k^{\alpha}}{Q_{n}}S_{k}f.

It is obvious that
\frac{n}{2}\log^{\left(\beta\right)}\frac{n^{\alpha}}{2^{\alpha}}\leq Q_{n}\leq n\log^{\left(\beta\right)}n^{\alpha}\rightarrow 0,\text{ \ as \ }n\rightarrow\infty.

Let us define maximal operator of Nörlund and
T
means by

\displaystyle t^{\ast}f:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|t_{n}f\right|,

\displaystyle T^{\ast}f:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|T_{n}f\right|

The well-known examples of maximal operator of Nörlund and
T
means are maximal operator of Cesáro means, Nörlund anD Reisz logarithmic means which are defined by:

\displaystyle\sigma^{\alpha,\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\sigma_{n}^{\alpha}f\right|,

\displaystyle L^{\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|L_{n}f\right|,

\displaystyle R^{\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|R_{n}f\right|.

We also define some new maximal operators:

\displaystyle\kappa^{\alpha,\beta,\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\kappa_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}f\right|,

\displaystyle\beta^{\alpha,\ast}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\beta_{n}^{\alpha}f\right|.

1.5
Weak-type and strong-type inequalities and a.e convergence

The convolution of two functions

f,g\in L_{1}(G_{m})
is defined by

\left(f\ast g\right)\left(x\right):=\int_{G_{m}}f\left(x-t\right)g\left(t\right)dt\text{ \ \ }\left(x\in G_{m}\right).

It is easy to see that

\left(f\ast g\right)\left(x\right)=\int_{G_{m}}f\left(t\right)g\left(x-t\right)dt\text{ \ \ }\left(x\in G_{m}\right).

It is well-known that if
f\in L_{p}\left(G_{m}\right),

g\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right)
and
1\leq p<\infty,
then
f\ast g\in L_{p}\left(G_{m}\right)
and

\left\|f\ast g\right\|_{p}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{p}\left\|g\right\|_{1}.

(1.26)

In classical Fourier analysis a point
x\in(-\infty,\infty)
is called a Lebesgue point of integrable function
f
is defined by

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\frac{1}{h}\int_{x}^{x+h}\left|f(t)-f\left(x\right)\right|d\mu(t)=0

On
G_{m}
we have the following definition of Lebesgue point:
A point
x
on the Vilenkin group is called Lebesgue point of
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right),
if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}M_{n}\int_{I_{n}(x)}f\left(t\right)dt=f\left(x\right)\ \ \ \ \ a.e.\ x\in G_{m}.

It is well-known that if
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right)
then a.e point is Lebesgue point and

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}S_{M_{n}}f(x)=f(x),\ \ \text{a.e. on}\ \ G_{m}.

(1.27)

where
S_{M_{n}}
is
M_{n}
-th partial sums with respect to Vilenkin systems.

Let introduced the operator

\displaystyle W_{A}f(x):=\sum_{s=0}^{A-1}M_{s}\sum_{r_{s}=1}^{m_{s}-1}\int_{I_{A}(x-r_{s}e_{s})}\left|f(t)-f\left(x\right)\right|d\mu(t)

A point
x\in G_{m}
is a Vilenkin-Lebesgue point of
f\in L_{1}(G_{m}),
if

\displaystyle\lim_{A\rightarrow\infty}W_{A}f(x)=0.

In most applications the a.e. convergence of
\left\{T_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
can be established for
f
in some dense class of
L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right).
In particular, the following result play an important role for studying this type of questions (see e.g. the books
[
62
]
,
[
112
]
and

[
192
]
).

Lemma 1.1

Let
f\in L_{1}
and
T_{n}:L_{1}\rightarrow L_{1}
be some
sub-linear operators and

T^{\ast}:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|T_{n}\right|.

If

T_{n}f\rightarrow f\text{ a.e. \ \ for every }f\in S

where the set
S
is dense in the space
\ L_{1}
and the maximal operator
T^{\ast}
is bounded from the space
L_{1}
to the space
weak-L_{1},
that
is

\sup_{\lambda>0}\lambda\mu\left\{x\in G_{m}:\text{ }\left|T^{\ast}f\left(x\right)\right|>\lambda\right\}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{1},

then

T_{n}f\rightarrow f,\text{ a.e. \ \ for every }f\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right).

Remark 1.2

Since the Vilenkin function
\psi_{m}
is constant on
I_{n}(x)
for every
x\in G_{m}
and
0\leq m<M_{n},
it is clear that each
Vilenkin function is a complex-valued step function, that is, it is a finite
linear combination of the characteristic functions

\chi\left(E\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,&x\in E,\\
0,&x\notin E.\end{array}\right.

On the other hand, notice that, by (
1.6
), it
yields that

\chi\left(I_{n}(t)\right)\left(x\right)=\frac{1}{M_{n}}\sum_{j=0}^{M_{n}-1}\psi_{j}\left(x-t\right),\text{ \ \ }x\in I_{n}(t),

for each
x,t\in

G_{m}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus each step function is
a Vilenkin polynomial. Consequently, we obtain that the collection of step functions
coincides with a collection of Vilenkin polynomials
\mathscr{P}
. Since the
Lebesgue measure is regular it follows from Lusin theorem that given
f\in L_{1}
there exist Vilenkin polynomials
P_{1},P_{2}...,
such that
P_{n}\rightarrow f
a.e. when
n\rightarrow\infty.
This means that the
Vilenkin polynomials are dense in the space
L_{1}.

1.6
Basic notations concerning Walsh groups and Functions

Let us define by
Q_{2}
the set of rational numbers of the form
p2^{-n}
, where
0\leq p\leq 2^{n}-1
for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Any
x\in[0,1]
can be written in the form

x=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}x_{k}2^{-(k+1)},

(1.28)

where each
x_{k}=0
or
1
. For each
x\in[0,1]\setminus Q_{2}
there is only one expression of this form. We shall call it the dyadic expansion of
x.
When
x\in Q_{2}
there are two expressions of this form, one which terminates in
0\text{'s}
and one which terminates in
1\text{'s}.
By the dyadic expansion of an
x\in Q_{2}
we shall mean the one which terminates in
0\text{'s}.
Notice that
1\nsim Q_{2}
so the dyadic expansion of
x=1
terminates in
1\text{'s}.

If
m_{k}=2,\ \text{for all}\ k\in\mathbb{N},
we have dyadic group

G_{2}=\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}Z_{2},

which is called the Walsh group

Let
G_{0}^{*}:=\{x\in G_{2}:x=y^{*}\ \text{for some}\ y\in G_{0}\}.
Define Fine’s map
\varrho:[0,1]\rightarrow G_{2}
by

\varrho(x):=(x_{0},x_{1},\dots)

(1.29)

where
x
has dyadic expansion (
1.28
). Then
\varrho
is a strictly increasing,
1-1
map from
[0,1)
onto
G\setminus G_{0}^{*}
. Moreover, it is easy to prove that
\varrho
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\varrho(x+)=\varrho(x-)=\varrho(x)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x\in(0,1)\setminus Q\\
\varrho(x+)\varrho(x),\varrho(x-)=\varrho^{*}(x)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x\in Q\\
\varrho(0+)=0,\varrho(1-)=0^{*}.\end{array}\right.

(1.30)

Here
\varrho(x+)
represents the limit of
\varrho
at
x
from the right in the usual topology on
[0,1]
and
\varrho(x-)
that from the left.

Let
C(G_{2})
represent the collection of functions
f:G_{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which are continuous from the dyadic topology on
G_{2}
to the usual topology on
\mathbb{R}.
Let
C_{w}
represent the collection of functions
g:[0,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which are continuous at every dyadic irrational, continuous from the right on
[0,1)
and have a finite limit from the left on
(0,1]
all this in the usual topology.

We shall call the map
f\rightarrow f\circ\varrho
the canonical isomorphism. It is easy to see that this map is a vector space isomorphism from
C(G_{2})
onto
C_{w}.
First, it is clear by (
1.30
) that if
f\in C(G_{2})
and
g:=f\circ\varrho
then

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}g(x+)=g(x-)=g(x)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x\in(0,1)\setminus Q_{2}\\
g(x+)g(x),g(x-)=f(\varrho^{*}(x))\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ x\in Q_{2}\\
g(0+)=f(0),g(1-)=f(0^{*}).\end{array}\right.

(1.31)

Thus the canonical isomorphism takes
C(G_{2})
into
C_{w}.
Next, notice by construction that the canonical isomorphism is a vector space homomorphism, i.e., preserves function addition and scalar multiplication. Finally, if
g\in C_{w}
then the map
f
defined on
G_{2}
by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f(y):=g(x)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ y=\varrho(x),\ \ x\in(0,1)\setminus Q_{2}\\
f(y^{*}):=g(x-)\ \ \ \ \ y=\varrho(x),\ \ \ \ x\in Q_{2}\\
f(0^{*})=g(1-)\end{array}\right.

(1.32)

is continuous on
G_{2}.

It is also easy to see that the canonical isomorphism takes the character system
\hat{G_{2}}
onto the Walsh system
w.
Indeed if
x\in[0,1)
has dyadic expansion (
1.28
) then the definition of the Rademacher functions given in 1.1 can be rewritten as

\upsilon_{n}(x)=(-1)^{x_{n}}\ \ \ \ \ \ (n\in\mathbb{N})

\rho_{n}(x):=(-1)^{x_{n}}

(1.33)

Comparing this with (
1.33
), we see that
\upsilon_{n}=\rho_{n}\circ\varrho
on
[0,1)
and
\rho_{n}(x)=v_{n}(\left|x\right|)
for
x\in G_{2}\setminus G_{0}^{*}
and every
n\in\mathbb{N}.

\phi_{n}:=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}\upsilon_{k}^{n_{k}}={\upsilon_{\left|n\right|}}\left(x\right)\left(-1\right)^{\underset{k=0}{\overset{\left|n\right|-1}{\sum}}n_{k}x_{k}}\text{\hskip 20.00003pt}\left(n\in\mathbb{N}\right),

(1.34)

w_{n}:=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}\rho_{k}^{n_{k}}

(1.35)

It follows from (
1.34
) and (
1.35
) that

\phi_{n}=w_{n}\circ\varrho

and

w_{n}(x)=\phi_{n}(|x|)\ \ \ \ \ \ (x\in G_{2}\setminus G_{0}^{*})

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}.

Fine’s map can be used to define a new addition and a new topology on
[0,1)
which are closely related to those on
G.
Indeed, define the dyadic sum of two numbers
x,y\in[0,1)
and the dyadic distance between these numbers by

x\overset{\cdot}{+}y=|\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)|

In terms of the dyadic expansions of
x
and
y
we have

x\overset{\cdot}{+}y=\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}{{}}\left|{{x}_{k}}-{{y}_{k}}\right|{{2}^{-k-1}}

Hence
\overset{\cdot}{+}
is evidently a metric and a commutative binary operation on
[0,1)
which satisfies
x\overset{\cdot}{+}x=0.
We shall call the topology generated by
\overset{\cdot}{+}
on
[0,1)
the dyadic topology. Note,
[0,1)
is not a group under
\overset{\cdot}{+}.

The Walsh functions almost behave like characters with respect to dyadic addition, namely,

\phi_{n}(x\overset{\cdot}{+}y)=\phi_{n}(x)\phi_{n}(y)\ \ \ \ \ (n\in\mathbb{N},\ \ x,y\in[0,1),x\overset{\cdot}{+}y\notin Q_{2}).

(1.36)

To prove (
1.36
) fix
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
x,y\in[0,1).
Notice that

\upsilon_{m}(x)\upsilon_{m}(y)=\rho_{m}\circ\varrho(x)\rho_{m}\circ\varrho(y)=\rho_{m}(\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)),

and that

\upsilon_{m}(x\overset{\cdot}{+}y)=\rho_{m}\left(\varrho\left(\left|\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)\right|\right)\right).

Since
\left|\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)\right|
is a dyadic irrational when
x\overset{\cdot}{+}y
is, it is clear that

\varrho\left(\left|\varrho(x)+\varrho(y)\right|\right)

for
x\overset{\cdot}{+}y\notin Q_{2}.
Consequently, (
1.36
) holds for the Rademacher case, i.e., for
n=2^{m}.
But the general case follows immediately since Walsh functions are finite products of Rademacher functions. Since for each fixed
y\in[0,1)
the set of points
x
which satisfy
x\overset{\cdot}{+}y\in Q_{2}
is a countable set, we observe that (
1.36
) holds for a.e.
x,y\in[0,1).

By a dyadic interval in
[0,1)
we shall always mean an interval of the form

I(p,n):=\left[p2^{-n},(p+1)2^{-n},\right)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ (0\leq p<2^{n},n,p,\in\mathbb{N}).

(1.37)

Clearly, the dyadic topology is generated by the collection of dyadic intervals. Moreover, each dyadic interval is both open and closed in the dyadic topology. It follows that each Walsh function is continuous in the dyadic topology. Thus the dyadic topology differs from the usual topology in an essential way.

For each
x\in[0,1)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
we shell denote the dyadic interval of length
2^{-n}
which contains
x
by
I_{n}(x).
Thus

I_{n}(x):=I(p,n)

where
0\leq p<2^{n}
is uniquely determined by the relationship
x\in I(p,n).
This is the same notation used for dyadic intervals on the group but will not cause problems because context will make it clear whether the dyadic interval is in the group or inside the unit interval.

A function
I:[0,1)\rightarrow R
which is continuous from the dyadic topology to the usual topology will be called
W-continuou
. Since

\left|x-y\right|\leq x\overset{\cdot}{+}y\ \ \ (x,y\in[0,1)),

it is clear that every classically continuous function on
[0,1)
is
W-continuous.
In fact, every function in
C_{w}
is unifoflIuy
W-continuous
on the unit interval. On the other hand, not every
W-continuous
function belongs to
C_{w}.

Let
L^{0}
represent the collection of a.e. finite, Lebesgue measurable functions from
[0,1)
into
[-\infty,\infty]
. For
0<p<\infty
let
L^{p}
represent the collection of
f\in L^{0}
for which

\|f\|_{p}:=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left|f\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p}

is finite. Let
L^{\infty}
represent the collection of
f\in L^{0}
for which

\|f\|_{\infty}:=\inf\{y\in\mathbb{R}:\left|f(x)\right|\leq y\ \text{for a.e. }\ \ x\in[0,1)\}

is finite. It is well known that
L^{p}
is a Banach space for each
1\leq p\leq\infty.

If
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{2}\right),
then we can establish the Fourier
coefficients, the partial sums of the Fourier series, the Fejér means, the
Dirichlet and Fejér kernels with respect to the Vilenkin system
\psi
(Walsh system
w
) in
the usual manner:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}^{w}\left(k\right)

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\int_{E}f\overline{w}_{k}d\mu,\text{\ \ \ \ \ \ }\hskip 20.00003pt\left(k\in\mathbb{N}\right),

\displaystyle S^{w}_{n}f

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\widehat{f}\left(k\right)w_{k},\hskip 20.00003pt\text{ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+},\ S^{\alpha}_{0}f:=0\right),

\displaystyle D^{w}_{n}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}w_{k},\text{ \hskip 20.00003pt\ \ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

We state well-known equalities for Dirichlet kernels (for details see e.g.
[
62
]
and
[
112
]
):

D^{w}_{2^{n}}\left(x\right)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}2^{n},&\,\text{if\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}x\in I_{n}\\
0,&\text{if}\ \ \ x\notin I_{n}\end{array}\right.

(1.38)

and

D^{w}_{n}=w_{n}\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}n_{k}r_{k}D^{w}_{2^{k}}=w_{n}\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}n_{k}\left(D^{w}_{2^{k+1}}-D^{w}_{2^{k}}\right),\ \ \text{ for \ }\ \ n=\overset{\infty}{\underset{i=0}{\sum}}n_{i}2^{i},

(1.39)

The most properties of
Lebesgue constants with respect to the Walsh-Paley system were obtained
by Fine in
[
31
]
. In (
[
112
]
, p. 34), the two-sided estimate

\frac{V(n)}{8}\leq L_{n}\leq V(n)

is proved, where

n=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}n_{j}2^{j}
and
V\left(n\right)
is defined by

V\left(n\right):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\left|n_{j+1}-n_{j}\right|+n_{0}.

If
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{2}\right),
then Fejér means and
Fejér kernels with respect to the Walsh system
\psi
(Walsh system
w
) is defined by

\displaystyle\sigma^{w}_{n}f

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}S^{w}_{k}f,\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle K^{w}_{n}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\overset{n-1}{\underset{k=0}{\sum}}D^{w}_{k},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right).

The
n
-th Nörlund logarithmic mean
L^{\alpha}_{n}
and the Riesz logarithmic mean
R^{\alpha}_{n}
with respect to the Walsh system
\psi
(Walsh system
w
) are defined by

\displaystyle L^{w}_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{S^{w}_{k}f}{n-k},\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle R^{w}_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{S^{w}_{k}f}{k},\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

respectively, where

l_{n}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{k}.

Kernels of Nörlund logarithmic mean and Riesz logarithmic mean are respectively defined by

\displaystyle P^{w}_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{D^{w}_{k}f}{n-k},\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right),

\displaystyle Y^{w}_{n}f

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{l_{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\frac{D^{w}_{k}f}{k},\text{ \ \ \ \ \ \ }\left(n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}\right).

1.7
Theory of Martingale Hardy spaces for
0<p\leq 1

The
\sigma
-algebra generated by the intervals

\left\{I_{n}\left(x\right):x\in G_{m}\right\}

will be denoted by

\digamma_{n}\left(n\in\mathbb{N}\right).

A sequence
f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)

of integrable functions
f^{\left(n\right)}
is said to be a martingale with
respect to the
\sigma
-algebras

\digamma_{n}\left(n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
if (for details see e.g. Weisz
[
174
]
)

1)\hskip 20.00003ptf_{n}
is
\digamma_{n}
measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N},

2)\hskip 20.00003ptS_{M_{n}}f^{(m)}=f^{(n)}
for all
n\leq m.

The martingale
f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)},n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is said to be
L_{p}
-bounded (
0<p\leq\infty
) if
f^{\left(n\right)}\in L_{p}
and

\left\|f\right\|_{p}:=\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\sup}\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{p}<\infty.

If
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right),
then it is easy to show that the
sequence
F=\left(E_{n}f:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is a martingale. This
type of martingales is called regular. If
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
f\in L_{p}\left(G_{m}\right)
then
f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)},n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is
L_{p}
-bounded and

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\left\|E_{n}f-f\right\|_{p}=0

and consequently

\left\|F\right\|_{p}=\left\|f\right\|_{p}

(see
[
92
]
). The converse of the latest statement holds also if
1<p\leq\infty
(see
[
92
]
): for an arbitrary
L_{p}
-bounded martingale
f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)},n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
there exists a function
f\in L_{p}\left(G_{m}\right)
for which
f^{\left(n\right)}=E_{n}f.
If
p=1,
then there exists a function
f\in L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right)
of the preceding type if and only if
f
is uniformly
integrable (see
[
92
]
), namely, if

\underset{y\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\sup}\int_{\left\{\left|f_{n}\right|>y\right\}}\left|f_{n}\left(x\right)\right|d\mu\left(x\right)=0.

Thus the map
f\rightarrow f:=

\left(E_{n}f:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is
isometric from
L_{p}
onto the space of
L_{p}
-bounded martingales when
1<p\leq\infty.
Consequently, these two spaces can be identified with each other. Similarly, the space
L_{1}\left(G_{m}\right)
can be identified with the space of uniformly integrable martingales.

Analogously, the martingale

f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)},n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is said to be
weak-L_{p}
-bounded (
0<p\leq\infty
) if
f^{\left(n\right)}\in L_{p}
and

\left\|f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}:=\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\sup}\left\|f_{n}\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}<\infty.

The maximal function of a martingale
f
is defined by

f^{\ast}:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|f^{(n)}\right|.

In the case
f\in L_{1}(G_{m}),
the maximal functions are also given by

f^{\ast}\left(x\right):=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{\left|I_{n}\left(x\right)\right|}\left|\int_{I_{n}\left(x\right)}f\left(u\right)d\mu\left(u\right)\right|.

For
0<p<\infty
the Hardy martingale spaces
H_{p}
consist of all martingales for which

\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}:=\left\|f^{\ast}\right\|_{p}<\infty.

Vilenkin-Fourier coefficients of martingale

f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
must be defined in a slightly different manner:

\widehat{f}\left(i\right):=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{G_{m}}f^{\left(k\right)}\overline{\psi}_{i}d\mu.

Investigation of the classical Fourier analysis, definition of several variable Hardy spaces and real Hardy spaces and related theorems of atomic decompositions of these spaces can be found in Fefferman and Stein
[
30
]
(see also Later
[
74
]
, Torchinsky
[
161
]
, Wilson
[
180
]
).

A bounded measurable function
a
is a p-atom if there exist an interval
I

such that

\int_{I}ad\mu=0,\text{ \ \ }\left\|a\right\|_{\infty}\leq\mu\left(I\right)^{-1/p},\text{ \ \ supp}\left(a\right)\subset I.

Explicit constructions of
p
-atoms can be found in the papers
[
20
]
and
[
21
]
by Blahota, Gát and Goginava.

Next, we note that the Hardy martingale spaces
H_{p}\left(G_{m}\right)

for
0<p\leq 1
have atomic characterizations :

The proof of Lemma
1.3
was proved by Weisz
[
174
,
175
]
.

Lemma 1.3

A martingale
f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is in
H_{p}\left(0<p\leq 1\right)
if and only if there exist a
sequence
\left(a_{k},k\in\mathbb{N}\right)
of p-atoms and a sequence
\left(\mu_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\right)
of real numbers such that, for
every
n\in\mathbb{N},

\hskip 20.00003pt\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mu_{k}S_{M_{n}}a_{k}=f^{\left(n\right)},\text{
\ \ a.e.,}

(1.40)

where

\hskip 20.00003pt\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left|\mu_{k}\right|^{p}<\infty.

Moreover,

\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}\backsim\inf\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\left|\mu_{k}\right|^{p}\right)^{1/p},

where the infimum is taken over all decomposition of
f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
of the form (
1.40
).

Explicit constructions of
H_{p}
martingales can be found in the papers
[
5
]
,
[
101
]
,
[
160
]
,
[
157
]
,
[
155
]
,
[
156
]
,
[
150
]
,
[
158
]
,
[
153
]
,
[
159
]
,
[
109
]
.

By using atomic characterization it can be easily proved that the following Lemmas hold:

Lemma 1.4

Suppose that an operator
T
is sub-linear and for some
0<p_{0}\leq 1

\int\limits_{\overset{-}{I}}\left|Ta\right|^{p_{0}}d\mu\leq c_{p}<\infty

for every
p_{0}
-atom
a
, where
I

denotes the support of the atom. If
T
is bounded from
L_{p_{1}}
to
L_{p_{1}},

\left(1<p_{1}\leq\infty\right)

then

\left\|Tf\right\|_{p_{0}}\leq c_{p_{0}}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p_{0}}}.

(1.41)

Moreover, if
p_{0}<1,
then we have the weak (1,1) type estimate

\lambda\mu\left\{x\in G_{m}:\text{ }\left|Tf\left(x\right)\right|>\lambda\right\}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{1}

for all
f\in L_{1}.

The proof of Lemma
1.4
can be found in Weisz
[
175
]
.

Lemma 1.5

Suppose that an operator
T
is sub-linear and for some
0<p_{0}\leq 1

\underset{\lambda>0}{\sup}\lambda^{p_{0}}\mu\left\{x\in\overset{-}{I}:\left|Tf\right|>\lambda\right\}\leq c_{p_{0}}<+\infty

for every
p_{0}
-atom
a
, where
I
denote
the support of the atom. If
T
is bounded from

L_{p_{1}}
to
L_{p_{1}},

\left(1<p_{1}\leq\infty\right)

then

\left\|Tf\right\|_{weak-L_{p_{0}}}\leq c_{p_{0}}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p_{0}}}.

Moreover, if
p_{0}<1,
then

\lambda\mu\left\{x\in G_{m}:\text{ }\left|Tf\left(x\right)\right|>\lambda\right\}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{1},

for all
f\in L_{1}.

The concept of modulus of continuity in martingale Hardy space
H_{p}

\left(p>0\right)

is defined by

\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right):=\left\|f-S_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{H_{p}}.

We need to understand the meaning of the expression
f-S_{M_{n}}f
where
f
is a martingale and
S_{M_{n}}f
is function. So, we give an explanation in the following remark:

Remark 1.6

Let
0<p\leq 1.
Since

S_{M_{n}}f=f^{\left(n\right)},\text{ \ for }f=\left(f^{\left(n\right)}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)\in H_{p}

and

\left(S_{M_{k}}f^{\left(n\right)}:k\in\mathbb{N}\right)=\left(S_{M_{k}}S_{M_{n}}f,k\in\mathbb{N}\right)

=\left(S_{M_{0}}f,\ldots,S_{M_{n-1}}f,S_{M_{n}}f,S_{M_{n}}f,\ldots\right)

=\left(f^{\left(0\right)},\ldots,f^{\left(n-1\right)},f^{\left(n\right)},f^{\left(n\right)},\ldots\right)

we obtain that

f-S_{M_{n}}f=\left(f^{\left(k\right)}-S_{M_{k}}f:k\in\mathbb{N}\right)

is a martingale, for which

\left(f-S_{M_{n}}f\right)^{\left(k\right)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0,&k=0,.\ldots,n,\\
f^{\left(k\right)}-f^{\left(n\right)},&k\geq n+1,\end{array}\right.

(1.42)

Watari
[
169
]
showed that there are strong connections between

\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right),\text{ \ }E_{M_{n}}\left(L_{p},f\right)\text{ \ \ and\ \ \ }\left\|f-S_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{p},\ \ p\geq 1,\text{ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}.

In particular,

\frac{1}{2}\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)\leq\left\|f-S_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{p}\leq\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)

(1.43)

and

\frac{1}{2}\left\|f-S_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{p}\leq E_{M_{n}}\left(L_{p},f\right)\leq\left\|f-S_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{p}.

Remark 1.7

Since

\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}\sim\left\|f\right\|_{p},

when
p>1
, by applying (
1.43
), we obtain that

\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)\sim\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right).

Next lemma gives answer what happens when
p>1
. The proof can be found in Neveu
[
92
]
(see also Weisz
[
178
]
).

Lemma 1.8

Let
p>1.
Then

H_{p}\sim L_{p}.

The proof of Lemma
1.9
can be found in
[
175
]
(see also book
[
112
]
).

Lemma 1.9

If
f\in L_{1},
then the sequence
F:=\left(S_{M_{n}}f:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is a martingale and

\left\|F\right\|_{H_{p}}\sim\left\|\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|S_{M_{n}}f\right|\right\|_{p}.

Moreover, if
F:=

\left(S_{M_{n}}f:n\in\mathbb{N}\right)
is a regular
martingale generated by
f\in L_{1},
then

\widehat{F}\left(k\right)=\int_{G_{m}}f\left(x\right)\psi_{k}\left(x\right)d\mu\left(x\right)=\widehat{f}\left(k\right),\text{ \hskip 20.44434pt}k\in\mathbb{N}.

2
Partial sums and Fejér means of Vilenkin-Fourier series on Martingale Hardy spaces

2.1
Some classical results on the partial sums and and Fejér means of Vilenkin-Fourier series

According to the Riemann-Lebesgue lemma (for details see e.g. the book
[
112
]
) we obtain that
\widehat{f}\left(k\right)\rightarrow 0,\text{ \ \ when \ \ \ }k\rightarrow\infty,

for each
f\in L_{1}.

It is well-known (see e.g. the books
[
2
]

and
[
112
]

) that if
f\in L_{1}
and the Vilenkin series

T\left(x\right)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}\psi_{j}\left(x\right)

convergences to
f
in
L_{1}
-norm, then

c_{j}=\int_{G_{m}}f\overline{\psi}_{j}d\mu:=\widehat{f}\left(j\right),

i.e. in this case the approximation series must be a Vilenkin-Fourier
series. An analogous result is true also if the Vilenkin series convergences
uniformly on
G_{m}
to an integrable function
f
.

By using the Lebesgue constants we easily obtain that
S_{n_{k}}f

convergence to
f
in
L_{1}
-norm, for every integrable function
f
,
if and only if
\sup_{k}L_{n_{k}}\leq c<\infty.
There are various results
when
p>1.

It is also well-known that (see e.g. the book

[
112
]
)

\left\|S_{n}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{p},\text{ \ when \ }p>1,

but it can be proved also a more stronger result (see e.g. the book
[
112
]
):

\left\|S^{\ast}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{p},\text{ \ when \ }f\in L_{p},\text{ \ }p>1.

Moreover, in the case
p=1
Watari
[
170
]
(see also Gosselin
[
61
]
and Young
[
186
]
) proved that there exists an absolute constant
c
such that, for
n=1,2,...,

\displaystyle\lambda\mu\left(|S_{n}f|>\lambda\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle c\left\|f\right\|_{1},\ \ \ f\in L_{1}(G_{m}),\ \ \lambda>0.

Uniform and point-wise convergence and some approximation properties of
partial sums with respect to Vilenkin (Walsh) and trigonometric systems in
L_{1}
norms was investigated by Antonov
[
3
]
, Avdispahić and Memić
[
1
]
, Baramidze
[
6
]
, Goginava
[
51
,
52
]
, Shneider
[
131
]
, Sjölin
[
137
]
, Onneweer and Waterman
[
93
,
94
]
.
Fine
[
31
]
derived sufficient conditions for the uniform convergence,
which are in complete analogy with the Dini-Lipschitz conditions. Gulićev

[
63
]
estimated the rate of uniform convergence of a Walsh-Fourier series by using Lebesgue constants and modulus of continuity. Uniform convergence of
subsequences of partial sums was investigated also in Goginava and Tkebuchava
[
55
]
, Fridli
[
32
]
and Gát
[
41
]
. Approximation properties of the two-dimensional partial sums with respect to Vilenkin and trigonometric systems can be found
[
112
]
and
[
192
]
.

A.e. convergence of Vilenkin-fourier series can be found in
[
110
]
. Divergence of Vilenkin Fourier series on the sets of measure zero and a.e can be found in Bitsadze
[
22
,
23
]
, Bugadze
[
24
,
25
]
, Fejér
[
35
]
Gosselin,
[
61
]
Kahane
[
66
]
, Katznelson
[
70
]
, Karagulian
[
67
,
68
]
, Kheladze
[
71
,
72
]
, Lebesgue
[
77
]
Stechkin
[
134
]
Young
[
183
,
184
,
185
]
and Zhizhiashvili
[
191
]
.

Some estimates of Fourier coefficients and an absolute, point-wise and a.e. convergence and divergence of Fourier Series with respect to complete orthonormal systems were studied in Bochkarev
[
10
]
, Gogoladze and Tsagareishvili
[
58
,
59
,
60
]
, Kashin and Saakyan
[
69
]
, Oniani
[
97
]
, Tsagareishvili and Tutberidze
[
162
]
. Approximation of functions on locally compact Abelian groups was investigate by Ugulava
[
28
,
29
]
(see also
[
27
]
).

Since
H_{1}\subset L_{1}
, according to Riemann-Lebesgue theorem, it yields that
\widehat{f}\left(k\right)\rightarrow 0
when
k\rightarrow\infty,
for every
f\in H_{1}.

The classical inequality of Hardy type is well known in the trigonometric as well as in
the Vilenkin-Fourier analysis and was proved in the trigonometric case by Hardy and
Littlewood
[
64
]
(see also the book
[
26
]
) and for the
Walsh system it was proved in the book
[
112
]
. Some inequalities relative to Vilenkin-Fourier coefficients were considered in
[
98
]
,
[
122
]
,
[
124
,
129
]
,
[
141
]
,
[
179
,
172
,
174
]
.

It is known (for details see e.g. the books
[
112
]

and
[
174
]
) that the subsequence
S_{M_{n}}
of the partial
sums is bounded from the martingale Hardy space
H_{p}
to the Lebesgue
space
L_{p},
for all
p>0.
However, (see Tephnadze
[
144
]
) there
exists a martingale
f\in H_{p}

\left(0<p<1\right),
such that

\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\sup}\left\|S_{M_{n}+1}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}=\infty.

The reason of the divergence of
S_{M_{n}+1}f
is that when
0<p<1
the
Fourier coefficients of
f\in H_{p}
are not uniformly bounded (see
Tephnadze
[
143
]
). On the other hand, there exists an
absolute constant
c_{p},
depending only on
p,
such that

\left\|S_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\text{ \ }p>0,\text{ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(2.1)

Tephnadze
[
144
]
(see also
[
146
]
and
[
149
]
) proved that for every
0<p<1,
the maximal operator

\widetilde{S}_{p}^{\ast}f:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\left|S_{n}f\right|}{\left(n+1\right)^{1/p-1}}

is bounded from the Hardy space
H_{p}
to the Lebesgue space
L_{p}.

Moreover, the rate of the sequence
\left(n+1\right)^{1/p-1}
is in the sense sharp.

It follow that for any
0<p<1
and
f\in H_{p},
there exists an
absolute constant
c_{p},
depending only on
p,
such that

\left\|S_{n}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left(n+1\right)^{1/p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\text{ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

Blahota, Persson, Nagy and Tephnadze (
[
11
]
) proved that for any
0<p\leq 1
and a sub-sequence of positive numbers
\left\{\alpha_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
, satisfying the condition

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\rho\left(\alpha_{k}\right)=\varkappa<\infty,

(2.2)

the maximal operator

\widetilde{S}^{\ast,\vartriangle}f:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left|S_{\alpha_{k}}f\right|

is bounded from the Hardy space
H_{p}
to the space
L_{p}.

Moreover, for every
0<p<1\ \
and any sub-sequence of positive numbers
\left\{\alpha_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
satisfying the condition

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\rho\left(\alpha_{k}\right)=\infty,

(2.3)

there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p},\ \left(0<p<1\right)
such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|S_{\alpha_{k}}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}=\infty.

It follow that for any
p>0
and
f\in H_{p}
, the maximal operators

\widetilde{S}_{\#}^{\ast}f:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|S_{M_{n}}f\right|\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \sup_{n\in\mathbb{N_{+}}}\left|S_{M_{n}+M_{n-1}}f\right|

(2.4)

are bounded from the Hardy space
H_{p}
to the space
L_{p}.

We also obtain that if
p>0
and
f\in H_{p},
the maximal operator

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N_{+}}}\left|S_{M_{n}+1}f\right|

is not bounded from the Hardy space
H_{p}
to the space
L_{p}.

Tephnadze
[
144
]
(see also
[
146
]
and
[
149
]
) proved that for any
0<p<1
and
f\in H_{p}.
Then there exists an
absolute constant
c_{p}
depending only on
p
such that

\left\|S_{n}f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq c_{p}n^{1/p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}.

Tephnadze (
[
144
]
) proved that for any
0<p<1,

f\in H_{p}
and
M_{k}<n\leq M_{k+1}
. Then
there is an absolute constant
c_{p}
depending only on
p
such that

\left\|S_{n}f-f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq c_{p}n^{1/p-1}\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{k}},f\right).

This estimate immediately follows that if
0<p<1,

f\in H_{p}
and

\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}^{1/p-1}}\right),\text{ when }n\rightarrow\infty,

then

\left\|S_{k}f-f\right\|_{H_{p}}\rightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{as \ }\,\,\,k\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, For every
0<p<1
there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p}
, for which

\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}^{1/p-1}}\right),\text{ \ when \ \ \ }n\rightarrow\infty

and

\left\|S_{k}f-f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}\nrightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{when\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}k\rightarrow\infty.

Tephadze
[
154
]
proved that for any
0<p<1
and
f\in H_{p},
there exists an absolute constant
c_{p},
depending only on
p,
such that

\text{ }\left\|S_{n}f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq\frac{c_{p}M_{\left|n\right|}^{1/p-1}}{M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle}^{1/p-1}}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}.

Moreover, for every
0<p<1\ \
and
\left\{n_{k}:\text{ }k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
any an increasing sequence of nonnegative integers such that condition (
2.3
) is satisfied and
\left\{\Phi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
for any non-decreasing sequence, satisfying the condition

\overline{\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}}\frac{M_{\left|n_{k}\right|}^{1/p-1}}{M_{\left\langle n_{k}\right\rangle}^{1/p-1}\Phi_{n_{k}}}=\infty,

(2.5)

there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p},
such that

\underset{k\in\mathbb{N}}{\sup}\left\|\frac{S_{n_{k}}f}{\Phi_{n_{k}}}\right\|_{L_{p,\infty}}=\infty.

Moreover, if
0<p<1
,
f\in H_{p}
and
\left\{n_{k}:\text{ }k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be an increasing sequence of nonnegative integers, then

\left\|S_{n_{k}}f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}

holds true if and only if condition (
2.2
) is satisfied.

In
[
151
]
(see also
[
154
]
) was proved that if
0<p<1,

f\in H_{p}\ \
and
M_{k}<n\leq M_{k+1}.
Then there exists an absolute constant
c_{p}
, depending only on
p,
such that

\left\|S_{n}f-f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq\frac{c_{p}M_{\left|n\right|}^{1/p-1}}{M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle}^{1/p-1}}\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{k}},f\right),\ \ \ \left(0<p<1\right).

It follows that if
\{n_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be increasing sequence of nonnegative integers such that

\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{\left|n_{k}\right|}},f\right)=o\left(\frac{M_{\left\langle n_{k}\right\rangle}^{1/p-1}}{M_{\left|n_{k}\right|}^{1/p-1}}\right),\text{ as \ }k\rightarrow\infty,

then

\left\|S_{n_{k}}f-f\right\|_{H_{p}}\rightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{as}\,\,\,k\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, if
\{n_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be an increasing sequence of nonnegative
integers such that condition (
2.3
) is satisfied. Then
there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p}
and a subsequence

\{\alpha_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\{n_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\},
for which

\omega_{H_{p}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{\left|\alpha_{k}\right|}},f\right)=O\left(\frac{M_{\left\langle\alpha_{k}\right\rangle}^{1/p-1}}{M_{\left|\alpha_{k}\right|}^{1/p-1}}\right),\text{ as \ }k\rightarrow\infty

and

\limsup\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|S_{\alpha_{k}}f-f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}>c>0,\,\,\,\text{as\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}k\rightarrow\infty.

In Tephnadze
[
144
]
(see also
[
149
]
) was proved that for every
f\in H_{1},
the maximal operator

\widetilde{S}^{\ast}f:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}}\frac{\left|S_{n}f\right|}{\log\left(n+1\right)}\text{\ }

is bounded from the Hardy space
H_{1}
to the space
L_{1}.
Moreover, the rate of the sequence
\log\left(n+1\right)
is in the sense sharp.
Hence, for any
f\in H_{1},
there exists an absolute
constant
c,
such that

\left\|S_{n}f\right\|_{1}\leq c\log\left(n+1\right)\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1}},\text{ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}.

(2.6)

This estimate immediately follow that if
f\in H_{1}
and
M_{k}<n\leq M_{k+1}
. Then there is
an absolute constant
c
such that

\left\|S_{n}f-f\right\|_{H_{1}}\leq c\lg n\omega_{H_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{k}},f\right).

By using this estimate we obtain that if
f\in H_{1}\
and

\omega_{H_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{n}\right),\text{ when }n\rightarrow\infty,

Then

\left\|S_{k}f-f\right\|_{H_{1}}\rightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{when\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}k\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover (for details see
[
144
]
) there exists a martingale
f\in H_{1},
for which

\omega_{H_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{2M_{n}}},f\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}}\right),\ \ \ \text{when}\ \ n\rightarrow\infty

and

\left\|S_{k}f-f\right\|_{1}\nrightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{when\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}k\rightarrow\infty.

In
[
151
]
(see also
[
154
]
) was proved that if
f\in H_{1}
and
M_{k}<n\leq M_{k+1}
, then there
exists an absolute constant
c
such that

\left\|S_{n}f\right\|_{H_{1}}\leq c\left(v\left(n\right)+v^{\ast}\left(n\right)\right)\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1}}.

Moreover, if
\left\{\Phi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be any non-decreasing
and non-negative sequence satisfying condition

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\Phi_{n}=\infty

and
\ \left\{n_{k}\geq 2:k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be a subsequence such
that

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{v\left(n_{k}\right)+v^{\ast}\left(n_{k}\right)}{\Phi_{n_{k}}}=\infty,

then there exists a martingale
f\in H_{1}
such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\frac{S_{n_{k}}f}{\Phi_{n_{k}}}\right\|_{1}\rightarrow\infty,\text{ as }k\rightarrow\infty.

In
[
151
]
(see also
[
154
]
) it was also proved that if
f\in H_{1}
and
M_{k}<n\leq M_{k+1},
then there
exists an absolute constant
c
such that

\left\|S_{n}f-f\right\|_{H_{1}}\leq c\left(v\left(n\right)+v^{\ast}\left(n\right)\right)\omega_{H_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{k}},f\right).

It follows that if
f\in H_{1}
and
\{n_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a sequence of non-negative integers such that

\omega_{H_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{\left|n_{k}\right|}},f\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{v\left(n_{k}\right)+v^{\ast}\left(n_{k}\right)}\right),\text{ as }k\rightarrow\infty,

then

\left\|S_{n_{k}}f-f\right\|_{H_{1}}\rightarrow 0,\text{ when }k\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, if
\{n_{k}:k\geq 1\}
be sequence of non-negative integers such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left(v\left(n_{k}\right)+v^{\ast}\left(n_{k}\right)\right)=\infty,

then there exists a martingale
f\in H_{1}
and a sequence
\{\alpha_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\}\subset\{n_{k}:\in\mathbb{N}\}
for which

\omega_{H_{1}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{\left|\alpha_{k}\right|}},f\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{v\left(\alpha_{k}\right)+v^{\ast}\left(\alpha_{k}\right)}\right)

and

\limsup\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left\|S_{\alpha_{k}}f-f\right\|_{1}>c>0\text{ when }k\rightarrow\infty.

Simon
[
119
]
proved that for any
f\in H_{p},
there exists
an absolute constant
c_{p},
depending only on
p,
such that

\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|S_{k}f\right\|_{p}^{p}}{k^{2-p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p},\text{ \ \ \ }\left(0<p<1\right).

In Tephnadze
[
141
]
) was proved sharpness of this result in the special sense. In particular, if
0<p<1
and
\left\{\Phi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be any
non-decreasing sequence satisfying the condition

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\overline{\lim}}\Phi_{n}=+\infty,

there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p}
such that

\text{ }\underset{k=1}{\overset{\infty}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|S_{k}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}^{p}\Phi_{k}}{k^{2-p}}=\infty.

In Gát
[
38
]
the following strong convergence result was
obtained for all
f\in H_{1}
:

\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|S_{k}f-f\right\|_{1}}{k}=0

For the trigonometric analogue see Smith
[
130
]
and for the Walsh-Paley
system see Simon
[
121
]
, for Vilenkin-like systems see Blahota
[
9
]
. Moreover, for all
f\in H_{1}
, there exists an absolute constant
c,
such that

\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|S_{k}f\right\|_{1}}{k}\leq c\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1}}\text{ \ \ \ and \ \ \ }\underset{n\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|S_{k}f\right\|_{1}}{k}=\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1}}\text{ \ }\left(n=2,3...\right).

In the one-dimensional case Yano
[
182
]
proved that

\left\|K_{n}\right\|\leq 2\ \ \ (n\in\mathbb{N}).

Consequently,

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{p}\rightarrow 0,\text{ \ \ \ when \ \ \ \ }n\rightarrow\infty,\text{ \ }(f\in L_{p},\text{ \ }1\leq p\leq\infty).

However (see
[
65
,
112
]
) the rate of convergence can not be better then

O\left(n^{-1}\right)

\left(n\rightarrow\infty\right)
for
non-constant functions. a.e, if
f\in L_{p},

1\leq p\leq\infty
and

\left\|\sigma_{M_{n}}f-f\right\|_{p}=o\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}}\right),\text{ \ when \ \ }n\rightarrow\infty,

then

f
is a constant function.

Fridli
[
33
]
used dyadic modulus of continuity to characterize the set
of functions in the space
L_{p}
, whose Vilenkin-Fejér means converge
at a given rate. It is also known that (see e.g books
[
2
]
and

[
112
]
)

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{p}

\leq c_{p}\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{N}},f\right)+c_{p}\sum_{s=0}^{N-1}\frac{M_{s}}{M_{N}}\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{s}},f\right),\text{ \ }\left(1\leq p\leq\infty,\text{ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}\right).

By applying this estimate, we immediately obtain that if
f\in lip\left(\alpha,p\right),
i.e.,

\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}^{\alpha}}\right),\text{ \ \ }n\rightarrow\infty,

then

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{p}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}O\left(\frac{1}{M_{N}}\right),&\text{if }\alpha>1,\\
O\left(\frac{N}{M_{N}}\right),&\text{if }\alpha=1,\\
O\left(\frac{1}{M_{N}^{\alpha}}\right),&\text{if }\alpha<1\text{. }\end{array}\right.

On the other hand, if
1\leq p\leq\infty,

f\in L_{p}
and

\left\|\sigma_{M_{n}}f-f\right\|_{p}=o\left(1/M_{n}\right),\ \text{as}\ n\rightarrow\infty,

then
f
is constant function
f=const.

Weisz
[
176
]
considered the norm convergence of Fejér means of
Vilenkin-Fourier series and proved that

\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\text{ \ \ }p>1/2\text{ \ \ and \ \ \ }f\in H_{p}.

(2.7)

This result implies that

\frac{1}{n^{2p-1}}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{p}^{p}}{k^{2-2p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p},\text{ \ \ \ }\left(1/2<p<\infty\right).

If (
2.7
) hold for
0<p\leq 1/2,
then we would have that

\frac{1}{\log^{\left[1/2+p\right]}n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{p}^{p}}{k^{2-2p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p},\text{ \ \ \ }\left(0<p\leq 1/2\right).

(2.8)

However, in Tephnadze
[
138
]
it was shown that the assumption
p>1/2
in (
2.7
) is essential. In particular, is was proved that there exists a
martingale
f\in H_{1/2}
such that

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{1/2}=+\infty.

For Vilenkin systems in
[
142
]
it was proved that (
2.8
) holds,
though inequality (
2.7
) is not true for
0<p\leq 1/2.

Some new strong convergence result for Fejer means was considered in Persson, Tephnadze, Tutberidze and Wall
[
102
]
.

In the one-dimensional case the weak type inequality

\mu\left(\sigma^{\ast}f>\lambda\right)\leq\frac{c}{\lambda}\left\|f\right\|_{1},\text{ \hskip 20.00003pt}\left(f\in L_{1},\text{ \ \ \ }\lambda>0\right)

can be found in Zygmund
[
192
]
for the trigonometric series, in Schipp

[
111
]
for Walsh series and in Pál, Simon
[
99
]
for bounded Vilenkin
series. Fujji
[
36
]
and Simon
[
120
]
verified that
\sigma^{\ast}

is bounded from
H_{1}
to
L_{1}
. Weisz
[
176
]
generalized this result
and proved the boundedness of
\sigma^{\ast}
from the martingale space
H_{p}
to the Lebesgue space
L_{p}
for
p>1/2
. Simon
[
119
]
gave a
counterexample, which shows that boundedness does not hold for
0<p<1/2.

The counterexample for
p=1/2
due to Goginava
[
48
]
, (see also
[
20
]
and
[
21
]
). In
[
138
]
Tephnadze proved that there exist a martingale
f\in H_{1/2}
such that

\sup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{1/2}=+\infty.

Moreover, there exist a martingale
f\in H_{p},
for
0<p<1/2,
such that

\sup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}=+\infty.

It follows that there exist a martingale
f\in H_{1/2}
such that

\left\|\sigma^{\ast}f\right\|_{1/2}=+\infty.

Moreover, there exist a martingale
f\in H_{p}
for
0<p<1/2,
such that

\left\|\sigma^{\ast}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}=+\infty.

Weisz
[
172
]
proved that
\sigma^{\ast}
is
bounded from the Hardy space
H_{1/2}
to the space
weak-L_{1/2}
. In
[
140
]
it was proved
that the maximal operator
\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}^{\ast}
with respect to
Vilenkin systems defined by

\widetilde{\sigma}_{p}^{\ast}:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\left|\sigma_{n}\right|}{\left(n+1\right)^{1/p-2}},

where
0<p<1/2
, is bounded from the Hardy space
H_{p}
to the Lebesgue space
L_{p}.

Moreover, the order of deviant behavior of the
n
-th Fejér mean was given exactly. That is, for any be any non-decreasing
sequence
\left\{\Phi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
satisfying the condition

\overline{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}}\frac{\left(n+1\right)^{1/p-2}}{\Phi_{n}}=+\infty,

we have that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\left\|\frac{\sigma_{M_{{}_{2n_{k}}}+1}f_{k}}{\Phi_{M_{2n_{k}}+1}}\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}}{\left\|f_{k}\right\|_{H_{p}}}=\infty.

As a corollary we immediately get that

\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left(n+1\right)^{1/p-2}\left(n+1\right)\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},

but it was proved more stronger result (for details see e.g.
[
149
]
). In particular,
if
0<p<1/2
and
f\in H_{p},
there exists an
absolute constant
c_{p}
, depending only on
p,
such that

\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq c_{p}n^{1/p-2}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}.

In
[
139
]
(for Walsh system see
[
49
]
) it was proved
that the maximal operator
\widetilde{\sigma}^{\ast}
with respect to
Vilenkin systems defined by

\widetilde{\sigma}^{\ast}f:=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\left|\sigma_{n}f\right|}{\log^{2}\left(n+1\right)},

is bounded from the Hardy space
H_{1/2}
to the Lebesgue space
L_{1/2}.

Moreover, for any
non-decreasing sequence
\left\{\Phi_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
satisfying the condition

\overline{\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}}\frac{\log^{2}\left(n+1\right)}{\Phi_{n}}=+\infty,

we have that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{\left\|\frac{\sigma_{q_{n_{k}}}f_{k}}{\Phi_{q_{n_{k}}}}\right\|_{1/2}}{\left\|f_{k}\right\|_{H_{1/2}}}=\infty.

As a corollary we get that

\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{1/2}\leq c\log^{2}\left(n+1\right)\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}.

but it was proved more stronger result (for details see e.g.
[
149
]
). In particular,
if
f\in H_{1/2},
there exists an
absolute constant
c,
such that

\left\|\sigma_{n}f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}\leq c\log^{2}\left(n+1\right)\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}.

For Walsh-Kaczmarz system analogical theorems were proved in
[
54
]
and

[
141
]
.

For the one-dimensional Vilenkin-Fourier series Weisz
[
176
]
proved that
the maximal operator

\sigma^{\#}f=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\sigma_{M_{n}}f\right|

is bounded from the martingale Hardy space
H_{p}
to the Lebesgue space
L_{p}
for
p>0.
Moreover, the
operator
\left|\sigma_{M_{n}}f\right|
is not bounded from the
space
H_{p}
to the space
H_{p},
for
0<p\leq 1.
This result for the Walsh system can be found in Goginava
[
51
]
and for bounded Vilenkin systems in the paper of Persson and Tephnadze
[
107
]
.

Approximation properties of subsequences of Fejér means with respect to the two-dimensional Walsh-Fourier series was considered in Persson, Tephnadze, Tutberidze
[
100
]
and Tutberidze
[
164
]
.

Tephnadze
[
108
]
proved that if
0<p\leq 1/2
and
\left\{\alpha_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be a subsequence of positive numbers such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\rho\left(\alpha_{k}\right)=\varkappa<c<\infty,

Then the maximal operator

\widetilde{\sigma}^{\ast,\vartriangle}f:=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left|\sigma_{\alpha_{k}}f\right|

is bounded from the Hardy space
H_{p}
to the Lebesgue space
L_{p}.

Moreover, if
0<p\leq 1/2\ \
and
\left\{\alpha_{k}:k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be a
subsequence of positive numbers satisfying the condition

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\rho\left(\alpha_{k}\right)=\infty.

then there exists an martingale
f\in H_{p}
such that

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\left\|\sigma_{\alpha_{k}}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}=\infty,\text{ \ \ }\left(0<p<1/2\right).

It immediately follows that for
0<p\leq 1/2,
and
f\in H_{p},
there exists an absolute constant
c_{p}
, depending only on
p
, such that

\text{ }\left\|\sigma_{n_{k}}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\text{ \ \ }k\in\mathbb{N}

if and only if

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\rho\left(n_{k}\right)<c<\infty.

As a consequence, for
p>0
and
f\in H_{p}
, then there exists an absolute constant
c_{p}
, depending only on
p,
such that

\left\|\sigma_{M_{n}}f\right\|_{p}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\ \ \ \left(p>0\right).

(2.9)

In
[
143
]
it was proved that if
0<p<1/2,

f\in H_{p}
and

\omega_{p}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}^{1/p-2}}\right)\text{when \ }n\rightarrow\infty,

then

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{H_{p}}\rightarrow 0,\text{ when }n\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p}

(0<p<1/2)
for which

\omega\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)_{H_{p}}=O\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}^{1/p-2}}\right)\text{ \ when \ }n\rightarrow\infty

and

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}\nrightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{when\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}n\rightarrow\infty.

When
p=1/2
we have the following results:
If
f\in H_{1/2}
and

\omega_{H_{1/2}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right),\text{ when \ }n\rightarrow\infty,

(2.10)

then

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}\rightarrow 0,\text{ when }n\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover, there exists a martingale
f\in H_{1/2}
for which

\omega_{H_{1/2}}\left(\frac{1}{M_{n}},f\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right),\text{ \ when \ }n\rightarrow\infty

and

\left\|\sigma_{n}f-f\right\|_{1/2}\nrightarrow 0,\,\,\,\text{when\thinspace\thinspace\thinspace}n\rightarrow\infty.

We state consequences of this result for Walsh system to clearly see difference of divergence rates for the various subsequences:
Let
0<p<1/2,

f\in H_{p}.
Then there exists an absolute constant
c_{p}
, depending only on
p
, such that

\left\|\sigma_{M_{n}+1}f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq c_{p}M_{n}^{1/p-2}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\text{ \ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}

(2.11)

and

\left\|\sigma_{M_{n}+M_{\left[n/2\right]}}f\right\|_{H_{p}}\leq c_{p}M_{n}^{1/2p-1}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}},\text{ \ \ }n\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.12)

Moreover, the rates
M_{n}^{1/p-2}
and
M_{n}^{1/2p-1}
in inequalities (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) are sharp in the same sense.

Blahota and Tephnadze
[
13
]
proved that if
0<p<1/2
and
f\in H_{p},
then there exists an absolute constant
c_{p},
depending only on
p
, such that

\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{p}^{p}}{k^{2-2p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p},

Moreover, if
0<p<1/2
and
\left\{\Phi_{k}\ :k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}
be any non-decreasing sequence satisfying the conditions
\Phi_{n}\uparrow\infty
and

\overline{\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim}}\frac{k^{2-2p}}{\Phi_{k}}=\infty,

(2.13)

there exists a martingale
f\in H_{p}
such that

\underset{k=1}{\overset{\infty}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{weak-L_{p}}^{p}}{\Phi_{k}}=\infty.

As a corollary we also get that if
0<p<1/2
and
f\in H_{p},
then there
exists an absolute constant
c_{p},
depending only on
p
, such that

\overset{\infty}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p}}{k^{2-2p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p},

\frac{1}{n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p}}{k^{1-2p}}\leq c_{p}\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p},

\frac{1}{n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f-f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p}}{k^{1-2p}}=0,

and

\frac{1}{n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p}}{k^{1-2p}}=\left\|f\right\|_{H_{p}}^{p}.

In Blahota and Tephnadze
[
13
]
also considered the endpoint case
p=1/2
and they proved that if
f\in H_{1/2}
then there exists an absolute constant
c
such that

\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{1/2}^{1/2}}{k}\leq c\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}^{1/2}.

As a corollary we also get that that if
f\in H_{1/2},
then

\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}^{1/2}}{k}\leq c\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}^{1/2},\text{ }

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f-f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}^{1/2}}{k}=0

and

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{\log n}\overset{n}{\underset{k=1}{\sum}}\frac{\left\|\sigma_{k}f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}^{1/2}}{k}=\left\|f\right\|_{H_{1/2}}^{1/2}.

2.2
Estimates of Dirichlet and Fejér Kernels with respect to Vilenkin systems

The proof of Lemma can be found in Tephnadze
[
143
]
.

Lemma 2.1

Let
x\in I_{s}\backslash I_{s+1},\ \ s=0,...,N-1.
Then

\int_{I_{N}}\left|D_{n}\left(x-t\right)\right|d\mu\left(t\right)\leq\frac{cM_{s}}{M_{N}},

where
c
is an absolute constant.

Proof:

Let
x\in I_{s}\backslash I_{s+1},\ \ s=0,...,N-1.

By combining (
1.5
) and (
1.7
)we
have that

\left|D_{n}\left(x\right)\right|\leq\underset{j=0}{\overset{s}{\sum}}n_{j}D_{M_{j}}\left(x\right)=\underset{j=0}{\overset{s}{\sum}}n_{j}M_{j}\leq cM_{s}.

Since
t\in I_{N}
and
x\in I_{s}\backslash I_{s+1},\ \ s=0,...,N-1,
we
obtain that
x-t\in I_{s}\backslash I_{s+1}
. By using the estimate above we get
that

\left|D_{n}\left(x-t\right)\right|\leq cM_{s}

and

\int_{I_{N}}\left|D_{n}\left(x-t\right)\right|d\mu\left(t\right)\leq\frac{cM_{s}}{M_{N}}.

The proof is complete.

The proof of the next lemma
2.2
can be found in Tephnadze
[
139
,
140
]
.

Lemma 2.2

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in I_{N}^{k,l},
where
k<l.

Then

K_{M_{n}}\left(x\right)=0,\text{ \ if \ }n>l.

(2.14)

and

\left|K_{M_{n}}\left(x\right)\right|\leq cM_{k}.

(2.15)

Moreover,

\int_{G_{m}}\left|K_{M_{n}}\right|d\mu\leq c<\infty,

(2.16)

where
c
is an absolute constant.

Next Lemma is proved in the book of G. N. Agaev, N. Ya. Vilenkin, G. M. Dzhafarly and A. I.Rubinshtein
[
2
]
.

Lemma 2.3

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}.
Then

n\left|K_{n}\right|\leq c\sum_{l=\left\langle n\right\rangle}^{\left|n\right|}M_{l}\left|K_{M_{l}}\right|\leq c\sum_{l=0}^{\left|n\right|}M_{l}\left|K_{M_{l}}\right|

(2.17)

where
c
is an absolute constant.

The proof of Lemmas
2.4
and
2.5
is due to Tephnadze
[
139
,
140
]
(see also Blahota, Tephnadze
[
13
]
).

Lemma 2.4

Let
x\in I_{N}^{k,l},\ k=0,\dots,N-2,\ l=k+1,\dots,N-1.
Then

\int_{I_{N}}\left|K_{n}\left(x-t\right)\right|d\mu\left(t\right)\leq\frac{cM_{l}M_{k}}{nM_{N}}.

Let
x\in I_{N}^{k,N},\ k=0,\dots,N-1.
Then

\int_{I_{N}}\left|K_{n}\left(x-t\right)\right|d\mu\left(t\right)\leq\frac{cM_{k}}{M_{N}},

where
c
is an absolute constant.

The next lemma is a simple consequence of Lemma
2.4
.

Lemma 2.5

Let
x\in I_{N}^{k,l},\hskip 20.00003ptk=0,\dots,N-1,\hskip 20.00003ptl=k+1,\dots,N.
Then

\int_{I_{N}}\left|K_{n}\left(x-t\right)\right|d\mu\left(t\right)\leq\frac{cM_{l}M_{k}}{M_{N}^{2}},\text{ \ for \ }n\geq M_{N},

where
c
is an absolute constant.

Now we prove some bellow estimate of Fejér kernel, wich will be used to prove some negative results. The proof of Lemma
2.6
is proved by Blahota and Tephnadze
[
13
]
.

Lemma 2.6

Let
t,s_{n},

1\leq s_{n}\leq m_{n}-1

n\in\mathbb{N}.
Then

\left|K_{s_{n}M_{n}}\left(x\right)\right|\geq\frac{M_{n}}{2\pi s_{n}},\text{ for }x\in I_{n+1}\left(e_{n-1}+e_{n}\right).

(2.18)

Moreover, if
x\in I_{t}\backslash I_{t+1},

x-x_{t}e_{t}\notin I_{n}

and
n>t,
then

K_{s_{n}M_{n}}(x)=0.

(2.19)

The proof of Lemma
2.7
can be found in Persson and Tephnadze
[
108
]
.

Lemma 2.7

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\left\langle n\right\rangle\neq\left|n\right|
and
x\in I_{\left\langle n\right\rangle+1}\left(e_{\left\langle n\right\rangle-1}+e_{\left\langle n\right\rangle}\right).
Then

\left|nK_{n}(x)\right|=\left|\left(n-M_{\left|n\right|}\right)K_{n-M_{\left|n\right|}}(x)\right|\geq\frac{M_{\left\langle n\right\rangle}^{2}}{2\pi\lambda},

where
\lambda:=\sup m_{n}.

For the Walsh system analogical of Lemma
2.8
was proved in Tephnadze
[
142
]
and Corollary
2.9
is simple consequence of it. Similar bellow estimate is proved in Blahota, Gát and Goginava
[
21
]
and
[
20
]
.

Lemma 2.8

Let

n=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\sum_{k=l_{i}}^{m_{i}}n_{k}M_{k},

where

0\leq l_{1}\leq m_{1}\leq l_{2}-2<l_{2}\leq m_{2}\leq...\leq l_{s}-2<l_{s}\leq m_{s}.

Then

n\left|K_{n}\left(x\right)\right|\geq cM_{l_{i}}^{2},\text{ \ \ for \ \ }x\in I_{l_{i}+1}\left(e_{l_{i}-1}+e_{l_{i}}\right),

where
\lambda=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}m_{n}
and
c
is an absolute constant.

Proof:

Let
